TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

  Foreign Policy | 04 Nov 20:56 UTC

  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Africa Brief. The highlights this week: A United Nations deadlock ends the 35-year-old peacekeeping mission in Western Sahara , local elections take place in South Africa , and Moscow tries to project an illusion of a successful Russia-Africa Summit . Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Africa Brief. The highlights this week: A United Nations deadlock ends the 35-year-old peacekeeping mission in Western Sahara , local elections take place in South Africa , and Moscow tries to project an illusion of a successful Russia-Africa Summit . A deadlock between the United States and Russia at the United Nations Security Council on Friday ended a 35-year-old peacekeeping mission in the disputed African territory of Western Sahara. The U.N. Mission for the Referendum in Western Sahara , known as MINURSO, was established in 1991 to monitor a cease-fire after a 15-year armed conflict between Morocco, which claims sovereignty over the territory, and the Algeria-backed Polisario Front, which seeks Sahrawi independence. It was also tasked with organizing a referendum for Sahrawi self-determination. The referendum has stalled for decades. Last month, a U.S. draft resolution for the Security Council sought to remove “referendum” from the mission’s name and make semi-autonomy under Moroccan sovereignty the sole basis for future political negotiations over Western Sahara. It would have also scaled back the peacekeeping force by April 2027. Russia vetoed the plan, instead putting forth a rival proposal to grant the standard one-year extension without imposing terms that favored Morocco. That, in turn, was voted down by the United States. The mission’s mandate subsequently expired on Sunday. Morocco annexed Western Sahara, a former Spanish colony, in 1975, displacing more than 100,000 people. An International Court of Justice ruling the same year found no historical basis for Morocco’s claim over the area. Rabat now controls around 80 percent of the territory , which boasts rich Atlantic fishing grounds and phosphate reserves. Currently more than 160,000 Sahrawi refugees live in camps hosted by Algeria. International aid groups have denounced the U.N. Security Council’s failure to renew the mission and find a solution to the severe humanitarian crisis caused by the political stalemate. Traditionally, Western powers maintained a neutral position on Western Sahara’s status. But in 2020, during U.S. President Donald Trump’s first term in office, Washington br
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/04/un-peacekeeping-mission-western-sahara-africa-morocco/

What’s more, it’s not just a rejection of tariffs, erroneously sold by Trump as a tax on foreigners, but a broad-based rejection of the notion that one man and his whims can hijack American wallets. According to one September poll , 70 percent of Americans believe that the president should seek congressional approval before levying new tariffs. Now, with a large and unassailable majority in both chambers, and with ever fewer Republicans bound in fealty to a lame-duck president , lawmakers have a real chance to claw back the constitutional power over trade and taxation that they have dribbled away over the last century. The message of the 2026 midterm elections is decidedly in: U.S. democracy is not dead, not by a long shot. There is, at long last, the glimmer of a beginning to the end of President Donald Trump’s decade-long dominance of national politics. Most of all, the 2028 presidential race—which effectively begins today—is now up in the air. So is the ultimate direction of the country’s international role, which will be decided by that election. But no one should kid themselves that the next two years are going to be anything other than ugly, chaotic, and brutal. Both Democrats and Republicans will be engaged in vicious infighting over fundamental policy issues, whether the question is the survival of the international order, engagement with allies and adversaries alike, immigration policy, or tariffs and the strength of the economy. Above all, it’s important to note that even with the dramatic turnover in Congress—with Democrats taking back both the House and Senate in decisive fashion—the president has shown very little inclination to defer to Congress, or even to the courts, on any issue that involves foreign or domestic policy. That Trumpian defiance will continue, whether the question is finding a way to end his prolonged Iran war on palatable terms, force a resolution to Russia’s nearly five-year-long invasion of Ukraine, persist in his crackdown on immigrants, or continue to unilaterally impose tariffs on whomever he pleases. In an Election Day interview with CBS News, Trump declared that even if the Democrats take control, he is prepared to use “tremendous” executive power to push through his agenda. “I have the ultimate power—it’s called ‘veto,’” he said. Trump also has the power of the executive order. In less than two years, he has signed 292 executive orders , 90 memoranda , and 192 proclamations , touching critical policies from voting rights to the reversal of 80 years of trade policy. * Q&A: What is the Brazil-led ‘deforestation roadmap’?
  Carbon Brief | 04 Nov 15:39 UTC
  On 3 November, the Brazilian presidency of the COP30 climate talks released its long-awaited “roadmap” on ending deforestation. Ahead of… The post Q&A: What is the Brazil-led ‘deforestation roadmap’? appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: On 3 November, the Brazilian presidency of the COP30 climate talks released its long-awaited “roadmap” on ending deforestation. Ahead of the Belém summit, Brazilian president Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva made a call for a “roadmap” to halt and reverse deforestation. Although this voluntary roadmap was outside the COP’s official agenda, it snowballed into a rallying point at the “Amazon” COP. By the end of the summit, 93 countries had signed on to the deforestation roadmap, committing to develop voluntary pathways to reverse forest loss and degradation by 2030. The Brazilian presidency called on countries to describe what a pathway away from deforestation could look like, what challenges and support they needed. This call was answered by 18 countries and four negotiating blocs that offered their inputs on the roadmap, along with scores of NGOs, research centres and other groups. Now that the roadmap has been published, Carbon Brief explains where it came from, what it entails, why some did not sign on – and what’s next. The “deforestation roadmap” was one of the signature announcements of the Brazilian presidency of the COP30 climate summit in Belém – albeit one that fell outside the official, negotiated outcomes. Brazilian president Lula first proposed the deforestation and forest degradation roadmap, alongside one on the fossil fuel transition , at the leaders’ summit in Belém a few days before COP30 began. By the end of COP30, nearly 100 countries had expressed their support for such a roadmap. This includes the EU, with its 27 member states, as well as the 56-member Coalition for Rainforest Nations . However, this was still short of the 196 parties needed for consensus at a COP, meaning that, ultimately, the roadmap was not a formal outcome. Instead, COP30 president André Corrêa do Lago announced during the closing plenary session that the presidency would work towards creating both deforestation and fossil-fuel phaseout roadmaps outside of the COP process. The deforestation roadmap is the latest in a series of international pledges to put an end to forest loss. In 2014, 39 countries pledged to end natural forest loss by 2030 at UN Climate Week, as part of the New York Declaration on Forests . Seven years later, at COP26 , more than 140 countries signed the Glasgow Leaders’ Declaration on Forests and Land Use . The declaration stated that countries “commit to working collectively to halt and reverse forest loss and land degradation by 2030” – although conf
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/qa-what-is-the-brazil-led-deforestation-roadmap

* Q&A: What is in the global ‘roadmap’ for transitioning away from fossil fuels?
  Carbon Brief | 04 Nov 15:23 UTC
  Transitioning away from fossil fuels is “urgent, inevitable and, yet, possible”, according to a first-of-its-kind global “roadmap” produced by Brazil.… The post Q&A: What is in the global ‘roadmap’ for transitioning away from fossil fuels? appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: Transitioning away from fossil fuels is “urgent, inevitable and, yet, possible”, according to a first-of-its-kind global “ roadmap ” produced by Brazil. The host of last year’s climate summit, COP30 , has set out a voluntary plan for how countries can move away from coal, oil and gas. The roadmap says there is no “one-size-fits-all” approach to transitioning away from fossil fuels and asks countries to “diagnose” their own “readiness” to start the process. After mapping out their specific circumstances, countries should then pursue “tailored levers” to take action, according to the roadmap. These efforts should culminate by 2033, it says – 10 years on from when the COP28 climate summit in Dubai called on all countries to contribute to a global transition away from fossil fuels. Below, Carbon Brief examines what the roadmap says and how it could influence debates about fossil fuels at the upcoming COP31 climate summit, which begins next week in Turkey. Calls for a global “roadmap” away from fossil fuels dominated debates at COP30, which was held in the Amazonian city of Belém. A group of 85 countries called for a reference to a new roadmap for transitioning away from fossil fuels to be part of COP30’s formal negotiated outcome. This call came the first “global stocktake” of action towards the world’s climate goals at COP28 in 2023 called on all countries to contribute to transitioning away from fossil fuels. However, another group of countries opposed such an inclusion. It was claimed that this group included 84 countries. However, Carbon Brief’s analysis of the “informal list” revealed inconsistencies and errors, meaning the total number of countries opposing the roadmap was likely smaller. After two weeks of fierce debate, Brazil announced that the fossil-fuel roadmap would not be part of COP30’s negotiated outcome – but that the presidency would work to create a roadmap outside of the UN climate process. This roadmap is voluntary and countries are not obligated to respond to its findings. At COP30, the presidency also announced that the roadmap would be informed by a first-of-its-kind conference on transitioning away from fossil fuels taking place in Santa Marta , Colombia. These talks, co-hosted by Colombia and the Netherlands in April, saw ministers and climate envoys take part in informal discussions about the barriers they face in transitioning away from fossil fuels. (See Carbon Brief’s full coverage of the talks.) As well as the fossil-fuel roadmap
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/qa-what-is-in-the-global-roadmap-for-transitioning-away-from-fossil-fuels

* CB Live: Carbon Brief’s team at COP17 on the nature talks’ key outcomes
  Carbon Brief | 04 Nov 10:40 UTC
  Following this year’s UN COP17 biodiversity conference, Carbon Brief’s journalists hosted a livestream to explain what happened at the summit.… The post CB Live: Carbon Brief’s team at COP17 on the nature talks’ key outcomes appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: Following this year’s UN COP17 biodiversity conference, Carbon Brief’s journalists hosted a livestream to explain what happened at the summit. Carbon Brief’s Orla Dwyer and Aruna Chandrasekhar have been on the ground in Yerevan, Armenia, over the past two weeks, covering the negotiations. They were joined on the livestream by associate editors Dr Giuliana Viglione and Daisy Dunne , who were following the talks from Washington DC and London, respectively. Carbon Brief has also published its 10,000-word explainer of COP17, which lays out the key outcomes in detail. Orla and Aruna kicked off the livestream by explaining the biggest issues at the event, including biodiversity finance and the first “global review” of progress towards reversing nature loss. The four journalists then took live questions from the audience in an “ask me anything” format, covering issues such as “ nature-harming subsidies ”, marine biodiversity and the status of the Cali fund , which is meant to raise private-sector finance. A recording of the webinar (below) is now available to watch on YouTube.
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/cb-live-carbon-briefs-team-at-cop17-on-the-nature-talks-key-outcomes

* How MIT Trained the Shah’s Nuclear Engineers
  New Lines | 04 Nov 13:26 UTC
  In 1975, MIT took the Shah of Iran’s money to train the country’s first generation of nuclear engineers, and the campus debate that followed, preserved in the university’s archives, anticipated the war being fought over that program today. The post How MIT Trained the Shah’s Nuclear Engineers appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: On June 6, 1974, Jerome Wiesner booked into room 1537 at the Royal Tehran Hilton in preparation for his audience with the Shah of Iran. The electrical engineer had just celebrated his 59th birthday. In his six decades, he’d been chief scientific adviser to JFK, a name on Richard Nixon’s list of political enemies, and was now the 13th president of the Massachusetts Institute of Technology (MIT). While his friends and family had mulled over what to gift him on his birthday, Wiesner was occupied by his own deliberations over what to gift the shah. “How about one of the crystals?” a colleague posited in a letter, referring to those grown aboard NASA’s first space station. “Two of them are said to be the most perfect crystals ever grown – because of the zero g environment, of course.” The MIT professors in possession of the perfect crystals might not be willing to part with an entire specimen, he admitted. “But I should think you might be able to persuade them to donate at least a millimeter slice.” Convinced, Wiesner arranged for a sliver of the space gem to be mounted on a velvet cushion. He was already in Tehran when an angry NASA official called MIT and mumbled about the upset at the Space Center. MIT was informed that “upper levels of NASA have ‘hit the ceiling.’” Turned out it wasn’t clear who actually owned the crystals, and Wiesner’s history as a thorn in NASA’s side didn’t help matters much. Mohammad Reza Pahlavi never received the perfect space crystal (gifted instead to President Gerald Ford a few months later), but Wiesner gave him something else at that meeting in Tehran that would end up being far more useful and far more polarizing. In 1975, MIT agreed to train two cohorts of 27 Iranian nuclear scientists in a three-year graduate program that would provide them with the skills necessary to run the reactors in Iran’s fledgling nuclear industry. The students were handpicked by the shah’s new atomic energy chief and had all expenses paid. At MIT, the special program caused an uproar that eventually receded into history, packed into numbered boxes in the university’s archives. That’s where I found them: the letters, speeches, reports from student journalists, transcripts from heated faculty meetings, notes from impassioned student activists, scribbled memos from secretaries — records of a debate that underscored a fundamental question: Who “deserves” technology? It was shared and reposted widely on WhatsApp and Telegram; his name dominated discussion on X and Facebook; and users translated the clip into several languages, including English, French and Spanish, bringing it to a much wider audience. Fidaa’s fame did not come from nowhere. His way of presenting information and popularizing educational, moral and religious subjects has won him a wide audience, especially among young people. Alongside his digital activity, he has an academic background in control and automation engineering and holds a master’s degree from the Technical University of Kaiserslautern in Germany. His criticism of the Houthis carries added weight because of his popularity across the Arab world, which his support for Gaza has strengthened. Gaza is the cause that most shaped the Houthis’ image in the region, and it is hard for them to question his position on Palestine or accuse him of siding with Israel.   Longreads | 04 Nov 10:55 UTC
  "Can virtual fencing transform ranching in the region?"
  FULL TEXT: Barbed and wire fencing covers hundreds of thousands of miles of the American West. It is highly effective, and can be unsparing. It is also costly—”$15,000 per mile or more to replace,” Kylie Mohr writes, a devastating expense for any rancher who may’ve lost miles of boundary during a wildfire. Mohr’s feature on the dawn of virtual fencing is a stimulating history of manmade borders and the inexpert ways in which they are deployed; it’s also an optimistic bit of technology reporting on an innovation that may prove wildly beneficial for ranchers and their herds. About 620,000 miles of barbed wire and other fencing crisscrosses the West like lattices on a pie crust of an imprecise baker, dividing land and life. These fences enable people to dictate how public and private lands are grazed and how, or even if, migrating wildlife reaches its final destination. They are sometimes more trouble than they are worth, though. Just ask the ranchers who build and repair them. “Real fence is bullshit,” Lanie White said as she programmed the collars stacked in the bed of a nearby ATV. “It’s expensive, it’s damaging, and it fragments ecosystems.” “Can they outrun an uncertain climate future?” “Wyoming’s 3.5 billion-year-old geologic history reminds us that Earth is ever-changing.” “In response to youth suicides, teachers show students the power of headbanging at Fire in the Mountains festival.”

Soon to be behind us, too, is this year’s midterm election frenzy, during which Democrats and Republicans have scrambled to win over the country’s mythical median voter. And where better to stage that fight than in Ohio? Every couple of years, it seems, the state’s reputation as a kind of blank canvas lends it outsize political heft: Pundits and politicians throw various shades of red and blue at its citizenry, who, as anyone who’s lived here will tell you, neither seek nor particularly care for the attention. Ohio’s on-again, off-again popularity with the rest of the country makes sense, I suppose. Once a reliably “purple” place — just as likely to be among the electoral map’s red Republican states as its blue Democratic ones — it has seemed, over the last three presidential election cycles, to have swung decidedly right. * Believing and thinking
  Aeon | 03 Nov 11:00 UTC
  The separation of church and state has an unspoken psychological basis: we choose what we believe, but not what we think - by Neil Van Leeuwen & Jacob Eisler Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Fryeburg, Maine, US. Photo by Andrew Lichtenstein/Corbis/Getty Images The idea of separating church and state – now entrenched in many modern liberal democracies – is in some ways strange. After all, governments must keep themselves informed and inform their citizens. That’s why they turn to the science of meteorology to predict weather patterns, to economics to understand patterns of growth and decline, and to the medical sciences to improve public health. So why wouldn’t governments also keep close alliances with religious institutions to know what’s going on with God? Separating church and state appears strange from other perspectives as well. In the historical sweep of large-scale human societies, it’s a relative newcomer, often associated with the United States’ founding father Thomas Jefferson. In 1802, Jefferson asserted the principle of separation in a letter to the Danbury Baptist Association in Connecticut: We’re fans of Jefferson’s principle, one of us (Neil Van Leeuwen) being a philosophy professor who grew up in the Christian Reformed Church, and the other (Jacob Eisler) being a law professor who regularly attends synagogue. But Jefferson’s letter makes putting the principle into practice sound simpler than it is – as though governments could be entirely hands-off when it comes to people’s beliefs, or ‘opinions’. That, however, can’t be right: engaging with and shaping political, social and other kinds of beliefs is actually a core function of government. Public education guides what people believe about everything from mathematics to history to astronomy. Governments also shape beliefs for policy reasons. If fire department officials learn that people believe a building is safe when it isn’t, they will try to change what people believe about the building’s safety. Belief is also a category of legal interest. If a psychotherapist, for example, believes a patient is a danger to himself or others, the therapist is obligated by law to report that belief. All this means that Jefferson’s ‘wall of separation’ can’t sensibly prohibit governments from intervening on beliefs of any type whatsoever. Rather, Jefferson – along with advocates of the principle who came after him – must have presupposed that there is something distinctive about religious beliefs that makes it reasonable to give them a different legal status. But what is this distinctive thing? Is it that religious beliefs concern God? No, because Buddhism is a religion with its own beliefs, 
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-psychology-of-separating-church-and-state

He also signed her up for a Premium account hoping to kill the ads, which had been engineered around her fears, he tells me; one had already talked her into buying life insurance she didn’t need. * The self-making world
  Aeon | 02 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Long mocked and misunderstood, Lamarck’s radical idea that organisms shape themselves and their world is making a comeback - by Jessica Riskin Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: A pod of humpback whales making its way through Alaska’s Inside Passage. Courtesy of Far North Footage/Getty Images The French naturalist Jean-Baptiste Lamarck was 65 when his great treatise on the transformation of life was published. With Philosophie zoologique (1809), translated a century later as Zoological Philosophy , Lamarck intended to establish an entirely new science, one built on the idea that all living beings are constantly transforming themselves, from the tiniest microbes to the most complex mammals, gaining new forms and new abilities. It wasn’t the bodily structures of organisms that determined their ways of life, Lamarck said, but just the opposite: their ways of life in response to their circumstances gave rise to their changing bodily forms. ‘Can there be any consideration in natural history more important,’ he demanded, ‘or deserving of greater attention, than the one I have just set forth?’ (My translation.) But if you’ve heard anything at all about Lamarck, it’s probably how wrong he was about one animal in particular, the giraffe, and how this giraffe fiasco epitomises his wrongness about everything . Lamarck suggested that, by stretching to reach leaves in high tree branches, giraffes slightly elongated their necks, creating tiny changes that were then handed down to their offspring, accumulating over many generations to produce the long necks we see today. Around the turn of the 20th century, Lamarck’s giraffe idea became a laughing stock when biologists roundly rejected the notion that bodily changes undergone in the course of an individual lifetime could be inherited in the next generation. Illustration from Histoire naturelle, générale et particulière (1749-1804) by Georges-Louis Leclerc, Comte de Buffon. Courtesy the Linda Hall Library It wasn’t only his ideas about long-necked mammals that came under attack. Lamarck’s interest in giraffes was just a passing mention in the midst of his voluminous writings, which set out a revolutionary, field-founding theory: organisms, he claimed, transform themselves and, in doing so, they remake the world. That idea has drawn scorn for more than two centuries. Though Lamarck is often overlooked, his science was foundational to modern biology and informed the theory of evolution that Charles Darwin later developed. Darwin followed Lamarck in believing in the agency of living beings to transform themselves and their environment. But most people don’t know about Lamarck’s influence on Darwin b
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-self-making-world-of-jean-baptiste-lamarck

As fighting intensified across the front lines, an unusual number of mourning posts began appearing in my Facebook feed. Facebook remains Yemen’s public square, a place where people mourn their dead, celebrate milestones, document their lives and argue fiercely over the country’s politics. By early October, as the Houthis advanced deeper into government-held areas of Taiz province and approached the eponymous city itself, my feed had turned into an endless stream of obituaries. Parents grieved for their sons. Wives mourned their husbands. Friends wrote heartbreaking tributes to men they had known since childhood. As I began reading and collecting these posts, Facebook’s algorithm appeared to take notice of my interest. Each time I opened the platform, more photographs of the dead appeared, many shared by people who were not even on my friends list. Scrolling through Facebook began to feel like walking past a wall covered with photographs of the dead. “Hisham, the hero, my brother and beloved martyr, please come back, you troublemaker. My heart aches from your absence,” wrote Shehab al-Shehabi, a journalist, on Facebook on Oct. 13, sharing a photograph of Hisham, who looked barely out of childhood, dressed in a shirt, with a tan tactical vest. Unlike in Houthi-controlled areas, where information about fighters’ deaths appears to be tightly controlled and casualties are often announced through official funerals, families and friends in government-controlled areas openly share photographs, names and personal stories of those killed on the battlefield, making public expressions of grief more visible and spontaneous. * In praise of the dilettante
  Aeon | 30 Oct 10:00 UTC [also carried by: Longreads]
  A wayfaring cosmopolitan who never gets stuck, the dilettante is the right anti-hero for our hyper-specialised age - by Jay Miller Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Jean Cocteau, poet, artist, filmmaker, photographed in 1948 by Philippe Halsman. Courtesy Magnum Photos Why, of all people, defend the dilettante ? An amateur at best, a flighty rascal at worst: why take an interest in someone whose own interests are never more than a passing hobby? These are serious times – why take seriously someone for whom nothing is serious? Merriam-Webster defines the dilettante as ‘a person having a superficial interest in an art or a branch of knowledge’. Encyclopedia.com calls the dilettante someone who cultivates an interest ‘without real commitment or knowledge’. Cambridge settles for someone whose understanding ‘is not very deep or serious’. Collins gives us someone who ‘seems interested in a subject … but does not really know very much about it’. None of it is flattering. Clearly, the dilettante is no aspirational character. No university markets itself as training the next generation of dilettantes; no magazine runs a ‘dilettante of the year’ issue (except, perhaps, for the excellent Idler publication). He – for the dilettante is, historically and in popular imagination, so often a ‘he’ – is in essence an amateur, a role defined by wide interests and narrow expertise. Such is the reputation of the dilettante, assigned and maintained by a culture that treats a single-minded devotion to one pursuit as the only route to a meaningful life. Pick something and stick with it, we are told. It’s not so much an explicit campaign against dilettantism I’m challenging, but rather the diffuse yet relentless pressure to narrow one’s interests, to specialise, to gain expertise, to forgo breadth for the sake of depth. We hear it in the barrage of imperatives to follow your dreams or find your passion uttered at graduation speeches, in self-help guides, and similarly corporate-sounding pep talks. Under this scheme, the specialist is the hero and the dilettante is but a cautionary tale: the person who never committed, never finished, never got good enough at any one thing. But what if the scheme has it backwards? What if the dilettante’s perpetual movement from one thing to another is not a failure of nerve but a different and defensible way of being, one whose exploratory zeal turns out to be just what a hyper-specialised, over-optimised and all-too- serious world needs most? Then the dilettante would turn out to be not a capricious buffoon but a kind of modern anti-hero. That, I argue, is a dilettantism worth defending. F irst, let’s consider
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-the-dilettante-is-the-anti-hero-of-our-over-optimised-age

  FULL TEXT: On the far eastern edge of Cairo, on Oct. 27, 2026, the New Cairo Misdemeanour Court (NCMC) was the scene of two events that will shape the future of independent journalism in Egypt. Together, the two cases illustrate how authorities are targeting independent media through different legal mechanisms, from licensing prosecutions to national security charges. The scenes sum up the state of independent journalism in Egypt, amid a fraught political climate and mixed signals that swing between a security clampdown and the prospect of a loosening of restrictions. A clear pattern stands out: Official rhetoric warning of threats to national security has risen in lockstep with an escalating campaign against independent media.   FULL TEXT: When I was a boy in Syria in the early 1990s, I spent my summer breaks going to work with my father. I rode out with him to the farm and helped him run it. These three small towns on the outskirts of Damascus sit next to vast stretches of agricultural land. I remember one discussion in particular that my father and the neighbors often had. They would sit in circles, and each farmer would vent his resentment at how much farmland the Hafez al-Assad regime had taken from them in 1983, when I was 1 year old, under a law that allowed the state to seize private land for public projects — a law that the new authorities in Damascus continue to uphold today, despite street protests against it. That law is now the new government’s problem, amid growing popular frustration. In the nearly two years since the Bashar al-Assad regime fell, the interim authorities have signed two large real estate deals with Saudi developers on land in the western suburbs of Damascus that the old regime took from farmers in the 1970s and 1980s: Abyat Hills in Qudsaya and Sham View between Qudsaya and Dummar. In late September, the original owners marched on both sites and stopped the machinery. The government responded that the expropriations were legal and all requisite compensation had been paid. Even for people from the area like me, the protests and the public debate since the downfall of the Assad regime have revealed the scale of the issue in ways we had not previously understood. In the towns along the Damascus-Beirut highway alone, residents count tens of thousands of acres taken for state housing under two decrees signed by Hafez al-Assad in the mid-1970s, with thousands more in Dummar. The Assad regime used the expropriation law to build military bases, security branches and loyalist housing around the Syrian capital. Much of the land the regime took is still in the state’s hands, either fenced off or built on. The new authorities have inherited that issue, but, to the farmers of my father’s generation, they have so far shown no intention of correcting a basic injustice. My own family, my father and his three brothers, lost about 50 acres spread across all three towns. They had bought the land with their hard-earned money while their widowed mother was alive. Imagine that across the continent in Accra, MTN’s MoMo has also crashed, and business in Makola Market has all but stopped. With the growing number of cyberattacks caused by artificial intelligence , these are highly plausible scenarios. In both these cases, Safaricom, MTN, and the Kenyan and Ghanaian governments need an off switch, or the technical ability and know-how to stop a rogue AI agent from executing its code on their payment systems. The distinction that will ultimately matter is … whether AI can serve its purpose and be controlled locally. Currently, the conversation on AI agents revolves around China versus the U.S. , open versus closed models, and who will dominate the AI race. But in Africa’s capitals, the distinction that will ultimately matter is not whose product was used to build a system, or who dominates whom, but rather whether AI can serve its purpose and be controlled locally. So how can Africa build with agency, and with control over the code and makeup of its own systems, even though they are built on foreign technology? African countries are already building AI for digital transformation, as outlined in the African Union’s continental AI strategy and country-specific AI strategies. Policymakers are building AI infrastructure but rarely discuss how AI delivers economic growth in a safe environment. Developers across Africa are creating AI-powered innovations that sit on the continent’s digital infrastructure — chief among them is mobile money. These innovations bring new opportunities, but also new threats. In July, AI agents running inside an OpenAI evaluation attacked the production infrastructure of Hugging Face. In September, rogue OpenAI agents breached Australian government websites. Africa, the home of mobile money, which has brought financial inclusion to hundreds of millions, may be the next target. In countries like Kenya and Ghana, mobile money is not simply another digital service; it is infrastructure for everyday economic activity. * The physics of getting fired

  FULL TEXT: The senior partner didn’t waste any time. ‘I’m sorry, Zahaan,’ he said, ‘your services are no longer required at the firm.’ We sometimes called him the Burger King behind his back, because he ate a Whopper Meal at his desk every day. I’d never spoken to him before, and now I never would again. It was the summer of 2001. I was fresh out of university and nine months into my first job as a management consultant in London. My parents had allowed me to move back into my old room, where I routinely collapsed after getting home from the office well after midnight. Professional life was punishing, but I gave it my all. For a brief time, I felt like I was flying: at my first performance review, I was told I had ‘partner potential’. But then I got a phone call, telling me that one of the senior American partners wanted to meet me. That was unusual; but then again, I’d been working so hard and getting such good feedback, I figured it couldn’t be anything too bad. I was directed to an office on the first floor, several levels below where I usually sat. After he fired me, my ears buzzed and my chest tightened; I don’t remember much else, except being told that I shouldn’t even return to my desk. It became apparent that the location of the meeting had been chosen because it would allow me to take a single flight of stairs down to the ground floor. No one would need to see me, and I wouldn’t need to speak to anyone. The decision felt cruel and unfair. But more than anything, it seemed to violate the laws that for many years had been my way of making sense of the world: the laws of physics. Cause and effect, action and reaction. What followed was an unlikely journey – one in which the equations I thought I’d left behind at university became, unexpectedly, a companion through the messiest parts of life. I often felt like I was watching from the edges, trying to decode the rules of a game that everyone else grasped intuitively. The only thing that got me through was physics.
